Figure 25
:
roc
curves for train and test sets on the left, which show little difference.

roc
curves of the five test sets of the 5-fold cross validation on the right.

The performance of the model is evaluated using the receiver-operator-characteristic (
roc
) curves:
in this two-dimensional graph, the signal efficiency is plotted against the background rejection for all output values of the binary classifier.
The obtained
roc
curves for train and test sets are shown on the left-hand side in
Figure

25
.
The larger the area under the
roc
curve, the higher the signal efficiency and background rejection.
The trained network reaches area-under-curve values of
0.8693
and
0.8690
for train and test sets, respectively.
With little difference between the two, the model shows no sign of overtraining.
As an additional test of the stability of the network, a 5-fold cross validation is performed:
the input data is split into five equal sets, and for each possible combination among the five, four sets are used for training, whereas the fifth serves as a test set.
The resulting five models show almost no performance differences – as seen in the
roc
curves of their test sets in
Figure

25
on the right-hand side.

application to analysis.

With input variables purely related to the shower shapes of photon candidates, the
ppt
is a generic tool to perform binary classification of those candidates into prompt and hadron-fake photons.

t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
measurements, in particular in the lepton+jets final states, where a significant background contribution from processes containing hadron-fake photons is expected, benefit from such a classification tool.
The
atlas

t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
analysis using
36\text{\,}\mathrm{fb^{-1}}
of Run 2 data
[
1
]
used the
ppt
to increase separation between prompt photon candidates and hadron-fake photons.
In the combined
e\mkern-1.0mu\text{+jets}
and
\mu\text{+jets}
channels, the analysis predicts about
12\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
contribution from hadron-fake photons.

Figure

26
shows the distribution of the
ppt
output in the combined signal region.
The
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
signal category and those background categories with prompt photons, such as
W\mkern-4.0mu\gamma
, show a strong slope towards the right-hand side of the distribution, whereas the
Had-fake
category, the category with hadron-fake photons, is distributed equally over the shown spectrum.
The categorisation into events with prompt or fake photons uses
mc
-truth information and is done similarly to what is described in this thesis in
Section

10
.

Figure 26
:
Control plot of the
ppt
output in the combined
e\mkern-1.0mu\text{+jets}
and
\mu\text{+jets}
signal region.
The classification into prompt and fake photons is done similarly to what is described in
Section

10
.
With
ppt
-specific scale factors applied to the predictions, good agreement between data and
mc
simulation is observed.
The hatched uncertainty bands include combined statistical and systematic uncertainties including systematics assigned to the
ppt
.
Figure taken from Ref.
[
1
]
.

For a better comparison of the
ppt
response to prompt photons and fake activity,
Figure

27
shows the
ppt
response for prompt photons, hadron-fake photons and electron-fake photons.
The latter are electrons that fake photon signatures in the
atlas
calorimeters.
To allow a direct comparison of the shapes, each distribution is normalised to unity.
The ratio at the bottom of the figure confirms separation power of the
ppt
output for prompt and hadron-fake photons.
For electron-fake photons, that were not considered in the training of the tool, little separation power is observed to the prompt category as the neural network did not

?
learn
?
to pick up any differences between these two photon types.

Figure 27
:
Output of the
ppt
for three different event categories used in the lepton+jets signal regions: prompt photons, hadron-fake and electron-fake photons.
The categorisation is done similarly to what is described in
Section

10
.
Figure taken from Ref.
[
1
]
.

Although named
tagger
, which usually implies that the classification tool makes a class prediction based on a decision boundary in its output distribution, the
ppt
was not used as such in the
36\text{\,}\mathrm{fb^{-1}}
analysis.
Instead, the continuous distribution, as shown in
Figure

26
, was used as an input variable into another neural network that was trained at event level.
This event-level binary classifier was then used to obtain maximum separation between the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
signal and all background contributions and to extract a fiducial cross-section of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
process.
Among the input variables to the event-level classifier, the
ppt
showed the highest separation power between
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
events and events with hadron-fake photons.

As the
ppt
was purely trained on
mc
simulation, it relies heavily on the correct simulation of the lateral and longitudinal photon shower development to also perform well on
atlas
data.
This is of particular importance given the observed discrepancies between data and simulation in the lateral shower-shape variables that are mitigated by fudge factors, as detailed in
Section

6
.
In addition, although identical in their shower development for given kinematics, the kinematic distributions of photons in a
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
analysis are different from those used in the training of the
ppt
.
As
Figure

28
shows, the
ppt
responds differently to high-
p_{\text{T}}
photons than to those with low transverse momentum, and the
ppt
spectrum of central photons is different from that observed for photons with high
|\eta|
.
To account for all these effects, simulation-to-data scale factors are derived in three control regions of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
analysis to correct the
ppt
distributions in
mc
simulation to those observed in data.
One region is dedicated to estimating mismodelling of prompt photons, the other two account for discrepancies between simulation and data for hadron-fake photons.

Figure 28
:
Dependence of the
ppt
output on the transverse momentum and pseudorapidity of the photons used in the training.
The plots show the
ppt
response to prompt photons from the training dataset in three different bins of
p_{\text{T}}
and
|\eta|
.

A dedicated prompt-photon control region enriched in
Z\to\ell\ell\gamma
events is defined by requiring two charged leptons of the same flavour, but opposite charge, and their invariant mass must be between
60\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}100\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
.
No requirements on the number of jets are imposed.
The photon candidates must pass both the identification and isolation criteria of the signal region.
The event selections of the two hadron-fake control regions are similar to those of the lepton+jets signal region:
exactly one photon, at least four jets, one of which must be
b\text{-tagged}
with the
77\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
operating point of the
mv2
tagger, and either one muon or one electron.
To make an event selection that is orthogonal to that of the lepton+jets signal region, photons in this control region must
fail
the isolation requirements.
In addition,
p_{\text{T}}^{\text{cone20}}(\gamma)>$3\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
is required to further remove contamination from prompt photons.
As only shower-shape variables enter the
ppt
that are also used for the photon identification working points in
atlas
, and as those shower-shape variables are largely uncorrelated with photon isolation criteria
[
193
,
187
]
, little absolute correlation between the failed isolation criteria and the
ppt
is expected.
However, as the isolation-fail photons and photons in the signal region might have different kinematic distributions, a second hadron-fake control region is derived from the selection criteria of the lepton+jets signal region, where the photons must pass the isolation criteria, but
fail
the identification requirements.

The simulation-to-data scale factors from the control regions are then derived as follows:
the regions are split in slices of photon
|\eta|
and transverse momentum to account for differences in the
ppt
response to different kinematics.
In each slice, the total predicted events in
mc
simulation are scaled to match the event yields in data.
Then, the observed slopes between data and simulation are extracted.
Due to a remaining
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
signal contamination of approximately
7\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
, the isolation-fail hadron-fake control region varies the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
signal by
\pm 50\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
, and the scenario with the largest discrepancy between simulation and data is chosen.
The extracted slopes from the prompt-photon control region and the isolation-fail hadron-fake control region are applied as correction factors to those categories with prompt photons and hadron-fake photons in the signal region, respectively.
That is, the scale factors for hadron-fake photons are applied to the
Had-fake
category of
Figure

26
, and the prompt-photon scale factors to the signal category and to all background categories with prompt photons.
To estimate uncertainties conservatively on these two sets of simulation-to-data scale factors, they are switched on and off separately and the resulting effects are symmetrised and used as uncertainties on the scale factors.
In addition, the extracted slope from the identification-fail hadron-fake control region is applied as an uncertainty on the hadron-fake simulation-to-data scale factors.

Figure

29
, taken from Ref.
[
4
]
, presents a study of the impact of the
ppt
on the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
cross-section measurement performed with
36\text{\,}\mathrm{fb^{-1}}
.
As figures of merit, the plot shows both the signal-over-background ratio and the significance
S/\sqrt{B}
.
They are plotted as a function of the event-level classifier output, that was once trained with the
ppt
and once without the
ppt
.
The shown signal-over-background ratios and significances are calculated as if a cut were placed on the event-level classifier at the point of evaluation.
The significance shows strong improvements towards the right-hand side of the distribution when the
ppt
is included into the training.
For example, for a cut value of
0.9
on the classifier output, the scenario with the
ppt
gives about
S/\sqrt{B}\sim 90
, whereas the scenario with no
ppt
yields
S/\sqrt{B}<60
.
On the other hand, including the
ppt
introduces additional uncertainties, given as shaded uncertainty bands.
They become more apparent as the cut on the event-level classifier output is tightened.

Figure 29
:
Impact of the
ppt
on the results of the
36\text{\,}\mathrm{fb^{-1}}
analysis.
The plot shows the resulting both signal-over-background ratio and the significance
S/\sqrt{B}
for different cuts on the event-level classifier that uses the
ppt
as an input.
Figure taken from Ref.
[
4
]
.

While the lepton+jets channels of
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
production deal with significant contributions from processes with hadron-fake photons, the dilepton channels provide a much cleaner environment for measurements.
The cross-section measurement performed with
36\text{\,}\mathrm{fb^{-1}}
also used an event-level classifier in these channels, although with a focus on separating the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
signal from prompt-photon backgrounds, the most dominant source of background in dilepton final states.
With predicted contributions of only
5\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
from hadron-fake processes, the
ppt
was not included into that classifier.
Consequently, it was also not considered for the analysis of the very clean
e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
channel presented in the remaining chapters of this thesis – but it would, however, be of use again for future measurements in the lepton+jets channels.

Chapter 4
Simulation of signal and background

Although quality criteria and dedicated selection cuts can be applied to
atlas
data to increase the fraction of a certain process in the set of selected events, there is no inherent way to know the exact composition of the resulting set of events.
Simulated events from Monte Carlo (
mc
) generators are compared with
atlas
data and are used to estimate this composition and the strength of the signal in
atlas
data.
This analysis considers
mc
simulations of the signal process and of all background processes with significant expected contributions in a
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
event selection in the
e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
channel.
After event generation, the response of the
atlas
detector to these
mc
-generated events is simulated with
geant
4
[
224
]
, as available within the
atlas
simulation infrastructure
[
225
]
.
Because simulating the full detector response is computationally expensive, the fast-simulation package
atlfast-ii
(
afii
) is used for some of the
mc
-generated events, which parameterises hadronic showers in the calorimeters to speed up simulation.

To account for additional proton-proton interactions from the same and neighbouring bunch crossings, known as in-time and out-of-time pile-up, respectively, the hard-scattering events are superimposed with minimum-bias interactions generated with
pythia
8
[
226
,
227
]
.
These interactions use an
atlas
set of tuned generator parameters called
A3

[
228
]
and the
nnpdf
2.3
lo

pdf
set
[
106
]
.
They are then reweighted to match the pile-up conditions observed in data.
In practice, three independent sets of events are generated for each
mc
simulation and are reweighted to reflect the data-taking conditions in 2015–16, 2017 and 2018, respectively.
The three different classes of
mc
simulation are referred to as
mc
production
sub-campaigns
and are named
mc16a
,
mc16d
and
mc16e
within the
atlas
simulation infrastructure.
The samples of the sub-campaigns are produced with different event seeds, and can thus be combined to give a total prediction for the Run 2 data-taking period between 2015 and 2018.

This analysis uses two types of samples to estimate signal and background contributions with final-state photons: samples where final-state photons are generated in the simulation of the hard interaction, and those where photons are not explicitly requested for the final state.
The first are referred to as
dedicated
samples, the latter as samples
inclusive in photons
.
Dedicated samples with photons were generated for the signal processes, described in detail in
Section

7
.
Dedicated samples were also produced for
V\mkern-4.0mu\gamma\text{+jets}
final states, where
V
refers to both
W\mkern-2.0mu
bosons and
Z
bosons.
These and all other photon-inclusive background samples are summarised in
Section

8
.
Although not simulated in the matrix-element generation step, photon-inclusive samples might still contain photons from the hard interaction as photon radiation is also simulated by the showering algorithms, irrespective of the generated matrix element.
This causes problems if a corresponding dedicated sample with photons from the matrix element is used simultaneously.
To avoid possible double-counting of events with photons, an overlap-removal procedure is applied between dedicated and photon-inclusive samples, as detailed in
Section

9
.

To distinguish photons from the hard-scattering event, referred to as
prompt
photons, from non-prompt photons and from other objects faking photon signatures,
mc
generator information is used to identify the true source of a reconstructed photon candidate.
This is further detailed in
Section

10
.

7
Dedicated simulations of the signal processes

The fixed-order theory calculations by
[
30
]

[
30
,
31
]
, used as a reference for this analysis, make
nlo
predictions for
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
cross-sections in the
e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
final state, and all off-shell effects are taken into account in this calculation.
Thus, more precisely, the simulated process is
W\mkern-2.0mub\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-2.0mub\gamma
production, which also includes singly-resonant and non-resonant diagrams with one or no top-quark mass resonances.
In an attempt to classify
W\mkern-2.0mub\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-2.0mub\gamma
production into two separate contributions, the doubly-resonant diagrams would correspond to pure
p\mkern-1.0mup\to t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
production, and the singly-resonant diagrams to
p\mkern-1.0mup\to t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-2.0mub\gamma
production – for now neglecting any interference effects between the two.
A naïve approach for simulating doubly-resonant contributions to the
W\mkern-2.0mub\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-2.0mub\gamma
final state would be to generate
p\mkern-1.0mup\to t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
events at leading order in
qcd
, for example with the
madgraph
5
_
a
mc
@
nlo
framework
[
229
]
, interfaced with a parton shower (
lo
+
ps
).
However, as diagrams with fewer resonances and associated interference effects are neglected, such a simulation would be insufficient to estimate the
W\mkern-2.0mub\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-2.0mub\gamma
fraction in data if a genuine comparison with the fixed-order calculation is desired.
An additional
lo
+
ps
simulation of
pp\to t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-2.0mub\gamma
would add singly-resonant diagrams and could help to complete the picture if combined with the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
simulation.
Examples of doubly-resonant and singly-resonant diagrams contributing to
W\mkern-2.0mub\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-2.0mub\gamma
production were shown in the previous chapter in
Figure

11
.
Unfortunately, generating large numbers of
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-2.0mub\gamma
events has proven technically challenging and was not feasible for this analysis.

Single top-quark production in association with a
W\mkern-2.0mu
boson and a photon (
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
), on the other hand, can be generated easily.

mc
simulations of
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
are used for this analysis to estimate singly-resonant contributions to the
W\mkern-2.0mub\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-2.0mub\gamma
process.
They were computed at
lo
in
qcd
in the
five-flavour scheme
, ignoring any mass effects of the
b\text{-quark}
and treating it as massless like the four lightest quarks.
In this scheme, the
b\text{-quark}
is described by parton distribution functions and it is considered in the perturbative evolution of
qcd
in the initial state, described by the
dglap
equations
[
72
,
70
,
71
]
.
As a consequence, and as opposed to the four-flavour scheme, the
b\text{-quark}
can enter the matrix element as an initial-state particle directly.
Hence, the leading-order partonic process for
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
production is simply
bg\to t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
, and problems in the definition of the
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
cross-section due to interference with
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
, as described in
Section

3
, do not occur.
However, the singly-resonant diagrams of
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
production only contribute to a
W\mkern-2.0mub\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-2.0mub\gamma
final state if an extra
b\text{-quark}
is present.
To add this
b\text{-quark}
to the matrix-element calculation, one would have to include
nlo
real-emission corrections, which add
p\mkern-1.0mup\to t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma[+X]
final states to the computation.
This is technically even more challenging than only a
lo
+
ps
prediction of
p\mkern-1.0mup\to t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-2.0mub\gamma
.
Representative Feynman diagrams of
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
and
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-2.0mub\gamma
production are shown in
Figure

30
.

However, even in a
bg\to t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
simulation, the initial-state
b\text{-quark}
needs to be generated through the
pdf
s
by simulating a
g\to b\bar{b}
split.
The second
b\text{-quark}
from this split acts as a spectator particle and corresponds to the missing
b\text{-quark}
of the
W\mkern-2.0mub\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-2.0mub\gamma
final state.
An estimate of this split through the
pdf
s
is far less precise than including the vertex of the split into the matrix element directly.
But, nonetheless, the
lo
+
ps

t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
simulation can be used to estimate contributions by singly-resonant diagrams to a
W\mkern-2.0mub\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-2.0mub\gamma
final state.
Therefore, as a

?
best estimate
?
of the
W\mkern-2.0mub\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-2.0mub\gamma
final state, the
lo
+
ps
predictions for
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
and for
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
are combined and treated as a single signal process in this analysis.
They are described in more detail in the following paragraphs.

Figure 30
:
Representative tree-level Feynman diagrams for
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
production on the left-hand side and
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-2.0mub\gamma
production on the right-hand side.
Diagrams such as the one on the left, with a
b\text{-quark}
in the initial state, can only be generated in the five-flavour scheme.
In the four-flavour scheme, an additional vertex is needed to produce the
b\text{-quark}
.
Thus, the right-hand side diagram is also the simplest possible diagram (i.e. leading order) for
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
production in the four-flavour scheme.

doubly-resonant production.

t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}
production in association with a photon in the matrix element is simulated in a dedicated sample using the
madgraph
5
_
a
mc
@
nlo
generator
[
229
]
at
lo
in
qcd
and the
nnpdf
2.3
lo

pdf
set
[
106
]
.
The matrix-element generation includes the decays of the top quarks and uses a filter to only produce final states with at least one charged lepton.
The events are generated as an inclusive, doubly-resonant
2\to 7
process, that is, the generator produces final states such as
p\mkern-1.0mup\to q\bar{q}bl\nu b\gamma
(lepton+jets channels) and
p\mkern-1.0mup\to b\bar{b}l\nu_{l}l\nu_{l}\gamma
(dilepton channels).
Thus, all diagrams where the photon is radiated by any of the top quarks, by the
b\text{-quarks}
, by the
W\mkern-2.0mu
bosons as well as by the decay products of the
W\mkern-2.0mu
bosons are included in the simulation.
Although not the dominant production mode, the final state can be generated through quark-antiquark annihilation.
Therefore, the
2\to 7
process also includes photons radiated by initial-state partons (
isr
).
To avoid infrared and collinear singularities due to the photon radiation, kinematic cuts are applied on matrix-element level.
Photons and charged leptons are required to have minimal transverse momenta of
15\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
, and their pseudorapidities must fulfil
|\eta|<5.0
.
In addition, the generated photons must not be in the vicinity of any other charged particle of the final state and at least be separated with
\Delta R>0.2
.
The top-quark mass in this and all other simulations is set to
172.5\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
.
The event generation is interfaced to
pythia
8 using the
A14
parameter tune
[
230
]
and the
nnpdf
2.3
lo

pdf
set to model parton shower, hadronisation, fragmentation and the underlying event.

evtgen

[
231
]
is used to simulate heavy-flavour decays, such as those of
B
and
D
hadrons.
The renormalisation and factorisation scales are dynamic and correspond to half the sum of all
transverse masses
of the final-state particles:

\displaystyle\mu_{R}=\mu_{F}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{f}\sqrt{m_{f}^{2}+p_{\mathrm{T},f}^{2}}\,,

where
f
runs over all final-state particles, and
m_{f}
and
p_{\mathrm{T},f}
are the rest mass and the transverse momentum of particle
f
, respectively.

singly-resonant production.

Associated
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu
production with an additional photon in the matrix element is simulated in two sets of dedicated samples using the
madgraph
5
_
a
mc
@
nlo
generator at
lo
in
qcd
and the
nnpdf
2.3
lo

pdf
set.
One set of events is generated as a
2\to 3
process and assumes a stable top quark and
W\mkern-2.0mu
boson.
Consequently, only diagrams of radiative
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu
production are considered, where a photon is radiated during the production of the top quark or the
W\mkern-2.0mu
boson, or from the initial state.
The decays of the top quark and the
W\mkern-2.0mu
boson are then simulated subsequently in the
madgraph
5
_
a
mc
@
nlo
framework.
A second set of events is generated as a
2\to 2\to 6
process, where a top quark and a
W\mkern-2.0mu
boson are first generated without extra photon.
Then, the top quark and
W\mkern-2.0mu
boson are decayed, and the photon is only radiated in this second step of the generation process.
Hence, this set only contains diagrams where the photon originates from the decaying top quark, from the decaying
W
boson or from any of the charged decay products of the two.
The two sets of
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
events are complementary and, once combined, provide a full simulation of the process.
As done for the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
simulation, the photons and charged leptons are required to have minimal transverse momenta of
15\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
, and their pseudorapidities must fulfil
|\eta|<5.0
.
The generated photons must again be separated from any other charged particle in the final state with
\Delta R>0.2
.
In both sets of events, the matrix-element generator is interfaced to
pythia
8, which uses the
A14
parameter tune and the
nnpdf
2.3
lo

pdf
set.

evtgen
is used to simulate heavy-flavour decays.

8
Background simulations

Events with
W\mkern-4.0mu\gamma\text{+jets}
and

programme

2.2.4 at leading order in

with the same

set.
All samples are normalised to the cross-sections given by the corresponding
mc
simulation.
The simulation in
sherpa
includes all steps of the event generation, from the hard process to the observable particles.
All samples are matched and merged to the

[

,

using the

[

,

.

in the matrix element are provided by the

]
.
These samples with matrix-element photons are paired with simulations of

and

final states, simulated with

2.2.1 at

in

.
The

3.0

These photon-inclusive samples are normalised to

[

.

using

.
The matrix-element generator is interfaced to

8 to simulate parton shower, hadronisation, fragmentation and the underlying event.
Heavy-flavour decays are modelled with

.
The matrix-element calculation uses the

3.0

[

]
.
The internal parameter

to control the threshold of the hardest emission is set to 1.5 times the top-quark mass following

The showering in

2.3

By applying a

++
2.0 programme at

in

, including soft-gluon resummation to next-to-next-to-leading-log order (see Ref.
[

]
and references therein), assuming a top-quark mass of
172.5\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
.
The resulting cross-section for
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}

\sqrt{s}=$13\text{\,}\mathrm{TeV}$
amounts to
\sigma(pp\to t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut})=$832\text{\,}\mathrm{pb}$
with remaining uncertainties due to scale and

Single-top-quark processes are modelled separately for three possible production modes, namely
s
-channel,

t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu
production, each of which are generated for top-quark and anti-top-quark production separately.
The three production modes are simulated on matrix-element level at

in

and the

The matrix-element generator is interfaced to

.

.
For the

is implemented to remove higher-order interference effects between

Events with two vector bosons, i.e.

,

.
The

The simulation includes all steps of the event generation, from the hard process to the observable particles.

.

[

]
.

9
Sample overlap-removal strategy

Generally speaking, parton-shower algorithms lack formal accuracy in the simulation of
hard
photon emissions, that is, emissions under large angles that carry considerable momentum fractions, due to the nature of their splitting function evolution.
Therefore, the radiation of high-
p_{\text{T}}
photons is simulated a lot more precisely in samples where these photons are generated at matrix-element level.
However, photons in the infrared/collinear limit, where their emission angles and momentum fractions become small, cannot be included in matrix-element calculations due to the occurring singularities.
Kinematic cuts are placed on the photons in the matrix-element generation step to avoid these limits.
As a consequence, events with photons below the kinematic thresholds can only be estimated from photon-inclusive samples.
To avoid double-counting of events with photons
above
the thresholds, sample overlap-removal techniques need to be applied between dedicated photon samples and photon-inclusive samples.
In particular, the removal procedures are applied for the two signal processes,
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
and
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
, and their photon-inclusive counterparts,
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}
and associated
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu
production.
In addition, sample overlap is removed between
W\mkern-4.0mu\gamma\text{+jets}
and
Z\mkern-2.0mu\gamma\text{+jets}
samples and the photon-inclusive
W\mkern-2.0mu\text{+jets}
and
Z\mkern-1.0mu\text{+jets}
samples, respectively.

The recipe for removing sample overlap is the following:
firstly, all events from the dedicated
X+\gamma
sample are accepted as the radiation of hard photons is simulated with much higher accuracy in these samples.
Additionally, the populated phase space of photon-inclusive samples is much larger than that of dedicated photon samples, and only a fraction of events includes photons.
In practice, if only events with a photon are selected, the photon-inclusive samples would have to be generated with many more events to reach the same level of statistical accuracy.
Secondly, events of the photon-inclusive
X
samples are removed if they fall in the phase-space region that is populated by the
X+\gamma
samples.
This
overlap
region is defined through the sets of cuts applied to the
X+\gamma
samples on matrix-element level, and consists of the following two requirements for all samples:

1.

p_{\text{T}}(\gamma)>$15\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
and

2.

\Delta R(\ell,\gamma)>0.2
,

where
\Delta R
is defined in the
\eta
–
\phi
plane in the
atlas
coordinate system.

Based on
mc
-truth information, the sample overlap-removal algorithm first compiles lists of photon and charged-lepton candidates generated at matrix-element level.
The candidates need to be genuine photons and charged leptons (requested through their
pdg id
values).
They must not originate from any interaction with the detector or from hadronic activity, but come from the primary interaction (required through their
barcode
and
truth origin
).
Additional criteria ensure that the found candidates are in their last and stable simulation states, or right before their decays in the case of muons and
\tau
-leptons (required through their
status code
).
Leptonically decaying
\tau
-leptons are considered as such and their decay products are vetoed on the candidate lists.
After the lists are compiled, photon candidates are dropped if they fail the above criterion on their transverse momenta.
For the remaining photon candidates, the
\Delta R
criterion is tested with all charged-lepton candidates, and the photon candidate is discarded as soon as it overlaps with any charged-lepton candidate.
If any photon candidates remain, the event is considered to fall in the overlap region, and it is vetoed for
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}
,
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu
,
W\mkern-2.0mu\text{+jets}
and
Z\mkern-1.0mu\text{+jets}
samples.

10
Categorisation of photon candidates

The main interest of this analysis are
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}
events where an additional photon is generated in the hard-scattering event.
These are referred to as
prompt photons
.
However, photons and other photon-like objects may occur at many stages of the simulation process, both in
mc
simulation and in the simulation of the detector response.
Some of the reconstructed photon candidates may also be other objects or activity recorded in the
atlas
detector.
Among the candidates detected and reconstructed with
atlas
, this analysis distinguishes three classes:

1.

Prompt photons
from the hard-scattering event,

2.

e-fake photons
, that is, electronic activity in the detector that fakes photon signatures in the calorimeters,

3.

h-fake photons
, that is, hadronic energy depositions in the calorimeters that fake photon signatures. This category also includes real, but non-prompt photons from hadronic decays of other particles, in particular from
\pi^{0}\to\gamma\gamma
decays.

To estimate the fractions of these categories in
atlas
data,
mc
-truth information is used to categorise photon candidates in simulation.
The
atlas
simulation infrastructure provides means to maintain an association between particles from the
mc
generation step and reconstructed object candidates in the detector.
Corresponding

?
mc
-truth particles
?
of photon candidates can be retrieved from the
mc
generator record, and quantities of these particles are used to categorise the photon candidate into the above classes.
The
atlas
software uses an internal classification scheme for
mc
-truth particles and introduces two parameters,
truth type
and
truth origin
, for this classification.

For a photon candidate to be classified as an e-fake photon, the associated
mc
-truth particle needs to be an electron or overlap with an electron with
\Delta R<0.1
.
For the latter criterion, a list of
mc
-truth electrons is compiled similarly to the charged-lepton candidate list described in the previous section, based on requirements on the
pdg id
, on the transverse momentum, on the centrality (
|\eta|
) and on the
barcode
of the
mc
-truth particle.
If any of the two e-fake photon criteria are met, the candidate is categorised as such and is not tested for any of the other two categories to avoid possible double-classification.

The remaining photon candidates are then tested against three criteria for h-fake photons, and any of the three is sufficient for the candidate to be accepted for this category.
Based on
truth type
and
truth origin
information, the photon candidate is categorised as an h-fake photon if the associated
mc
-truth particle
(1) is a photon and originates from a baryon or meson,
(2) is a photon and originates from a
\pi^{0}\to\gamma\gamma
decay or
(3) is a hadronic energy deposition.
Only if photon candidates meet none of the criteria for e-fake photons or h-fake photons, they are categorised as prompt photons.

Chapter 5
Event selection

With
mc
simulations for all major signal and background processes prepared, their predictions may now be compared with
atlas
data.
This analysis uses proton-proton collision data taken with the
atlas
detector in the years 2015 to 2018 during Run 2 of the
lhc
.
As summarised in
Chapter

2
, the total data delivered by the
lhc
amounts to an integrated luminosity of
156\text{\,}\mathrm{fb^{-1}}
, of which
147\text{\,}\mathrm{fb^{-1}}
, more than
94\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
, were recorded with the
atlas
detector.
The dataset is split in four subsets, one for each year of data-taking.
The subsets consist of data-taking
runs
that are often, but not always identical to the periods the
lhc
was refilled with proton beams.
Runs are divided in small, equal-length time units called
luminosity blocks
, for which the instantaneous luminosity is averaged to calculate a luminosity-over-time profile.
Depending on the internal clock signal of the
atlas
detector, one luminosity block corresponds to approximately one minute of data-taking.
Luminosity blocks are flagged if the
lhc
beams were unstable, if the
atlas
detector was not fully operational, or if the data recorded with
atlas
did not fulfil a set of quality criteria.
The remaining luminosity blocks of all runs are considered to be

?
good
?
for physics analyses and enter the
good-run lists
, one of which is compiled for each year of data-taking.
The dataset on the four good-run lists combined corresponds to an integrated luminosity of
139\text{\,}\mathrm{fb^{-1}}
.
More details for individual years are given in
Table

3
.
Unless analyses do not require all detector components to be online or have otherwise looser requirements, all
atlas
analyses base their event selection on these good-run lists.

Table 3
:
Delivered integrated luminosity of the
lhc
and the integrated luminosity of the
atlas
good-run lists for each year of data-taking during Run 2.

Year

Delivered
[$\mathrm{fb^{-1}}$]

Good-run lists
[$\mathrm{fb^{-1}}$]

2015

4.2

3.2

2016

38.5

33.0

2017

50.2

44.3

2018

63.3

58.5

Total

156.2

139.0

The aim of the analysis presented in this thesis is to select phase-space regions highly enriched in
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
events.
However, before tightening the selection to signal-like events in the
e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
final state, a more generic pre-selection of events is applied.
This reduces the amount of data drastically and only leaves events that are interesting for control studies and the measurement itself.
Among the most prominent features of the
e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
final state are the two charged leptons with high transverse momenta.
Therefore, events are required to have fired a single-lepton trigger in the
atlas

hlt
trigger system.
For both electrons and muons, not single triggers, but chains of triggers are defined, out of which only one must have fired.
The triggers in a chain differ in their
p_{\text{T}}
thresholds and identification and isolation requirements:
those with lower
p_{\text{T}}
thresholds require the trigger objects to be more tightly identified and isolated, whereas high-
p_{\text{T}}
triggers have looser requirements.
The full list of the considered triggers is given in
Table

4
following the convention:

\displaystyle\mathtt{HLT\_<type><pT>\_<ID>\_<iso>}\,,

where the
<pT>
tag defines the
p_{\text{T}}
threshold and the following tags describe the identification and isolation criteria.
Due to the increased average pile-up in later years of data-taking, the trigger chains were adjusted accordingly to not overload the
tdaq
system with too high event rates.
In addition to the trigger-fire requirements, an electron or muon candidate must be reconstructed according to the criteria defined in
Section

6
and matched to the candidate that fired the trigger.
To omit regions of low trigger efficiency
10
10

10

Due to detection inefficiencies and uncertainties in the energy/momentum measurements of the involved
atlas
components, triggers show a turn-on behaviour in trigger efficiency as the object candidate’s transverse momentum increases.
, the matched electron and muon candidates must fulfil the
p_{\text{T}}
requirements also listed in
Table

4
: at least
25\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}27\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}28\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}28\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
for 2015 to 2018, respectively.
The identical requirements are imposed on
atlas
data and
mc
simulation.

In addition, all events must have at least one primary vertex with at least two tracks with
p_{\text{T}}>$400\text{\,}\mathrm{MeV}$
matched to that vertex.
Then, the object candidates are reconstructed with the criteria listed in
Section

6
, but without imposing any identification or isolation requirements yet.
With these lists of candidates, the overlap-removal steps described in the same section are applied.
What follows is a procedure called
event cleaning
, in which all reconstructed jet candidates are tested against a set of quality criteria to distinguish them from non-collision background.
This includes beam-induced background due to upstream proton losses in the beam, showers in the detector induced by cosmic rays, and large-scale coherent calorimeter noise.
The
BadLoose
criteria, further described in Ref.
[
250
]
, are applied.
If any of the reconstructed jet candidates fails the requirements, the entire event is discarded because even low-
p_{\text{T}}
candidates would spoil the
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
calculation.

definition of the signal region.

After pre-selecting events, the identification and isolation requirements described in
Section

6
are imposed on all reconstructed object candidates.
Then, the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
signal region is selected as follows:
exactly one electron and one muon are required, each of which must carry
p_{\text{T}}>$25\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
.
Note that, regardless, one of the two charged leptons must fulfil the trigger-match requirements of the pre-selection and pass the corresponding
p_{\text{T}}
threshold.
Electron and muon must be of opposite electric charge.
In addition, the event is required to have at least two jets with
p_{\text{T}}>$25\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
, at least one of which must be
b\text{-tagged}
with the
mv2
discriminant at the
85\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
operating point.
The event must have exactly one photon with
p_{\text{T}}>$20\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
.
Although the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
final state contains two neutrinos and large missing transverse momentum is expected, no explicit cut on
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
is imposed due to the low background contamination of the selected signal region.

The selected events from
mc
simulation are then grouped based on their origin:
the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma\ e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
and
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma\ e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
categories contain events from simulated
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
and
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
events in the
e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
decay channel, respectively.
The decay channels are identified through
mc
-truth information for each event.
Dilepton events involving
\tau
-leptons, which subsequently decayed into other leptons, are counted separately as they are not included in the reference theory computation
[
30
,
31
]
.
Minor contributions are also expected from lepton+jets events with an additional lepton faked by hadronic activity.
They are combined with the dilepton events involving
\tau
-lepton decays and counted in the category
\textit{Other}\ t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma/t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
.
Selected events from background simulation are categorised based on the origin of the reconstructed photon, which is identified as described in
Section

10
.
The categories are:

1.

The
h-fake
category with any type of h-fake photon that passed the photon selection criteria.
The category is dominated by
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}
events with an additional h-fake photon in the final state, but it may also contain events with a prompt photon in the simulation, that failed detection or reconstruction, and an additionally reconstructed h-fake photon.

2.

The
e-fake
category with events that have no prompt photon, but an additional electron misreconstructed as a photon (e-fake photon).
Again, this may also include events with a prompt photon in the simulation, that failed detection or reconstruction, and an additionally reconstructed e-fake photon.

3.

The
\textit{Prompt}\ \gamma\ \textit{bkg.}
category with background-like events, where the reconstructed photon candidate is a prompt photon.

The predicted event yields of all categories and the numbers of reconstructed events in
atlas
data are listed in
Table

5
.
To allow a better comparison, the numbers are also given separately for 2015/16, 2017 and 2018.
The quoted uncertainties on the predictions are
mc
-statistical uncertainties.
While all background processes are simulated at
nlo
in
qcd
, or their cross-sections are reweighted to that order through
k
-factors, the estimation of the signal processes is only based on the
lo
cross-sections calculated in
madgraph
5
_
a
mc
@
nlo
and, thus, is not expected to be very accurate in its total rate.
11
11

11

The
atlas

t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
analysis using
36\text{\,}\mathrm{fb^{-1}}
performed dedicated calculations of
lo
and
nlo
cross-sections in the fiducial volume of the measurement, which yielded a
k
-factor as high as 1.44 in the
e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
channel
[
1
]
.

Therefore, for aesthetic reasons in control plots and to allow a better assessment of the event composition in
mc
simulation when comparing to data, the predictions of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
and
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
categories marked with (*) were scaled in such a way that the total
mc
prediction matches the numbers of reconstructed events in data in each column of the table.
However, this has no effect on the results of the measurement as the fiducial inclusive cross-section is extracted independently of the predicted cross-section as detailed in
Chapter

6
.

.

and

with h-fake photons or e-fake photons in the final state or phase-space regions not simulated in the

and
Z\mkern-2.0mu\gamma\text{+jets}
samples, which are expected to only contribute weakly to the chosen signal region.

The predicted event yields for the full Run 2 dataset show that the defined signal region is expected to be dominated by
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
and
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
contributions.
The
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma\ e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
and
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma\ e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
signal categories with a total of
2547
expected events account for
84.5\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
of the total
mc
estimate, which corresponds to a signal-to-background ratio of
5.45
.
This results in a statistical significance of the signal of
\nicefrac{{S}}{{\sqrt{B}}}=$117.9$
.
The combined total
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
and
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
prediction is composed of
90.1\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}

e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
-channel events and
9.9\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
non-
e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
-channel events.
Within these numbers, the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
-to-
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
ratio is
15.4
and
14.0
for
e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
-channel and non-
e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
-channel events, respectively.
The non-
e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
-channel contributions from
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
and
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
also constitute the largest background:
within the
15.5\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
background events, some
60\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
are classified as
\textit{Other}\ t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma/t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
.
The remaining background events are composed of
17\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
from the
h-fake
category,
5\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
from the
e-fake
category, and
19\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
from the
\textit{Prompt}\ \gamma\ \textit{bkg.}
category.

Within the
h-fake
category, the vast majority of events comes from
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}
simulation:
the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}
pair decays in the
e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
channel, and hadronic activity originating from an additionally radiated gluon mimics a photon signature in the
atlas
detector.
Of the
77.5
expected events in the
h-fake
category, more than
93\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
are of this type.
The
e-fake
category with
23
expected events has its largest contributions from
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}
, diboson and
t\mkern 0.5mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\mkern-1.0muV
simulation.
The latter two types of processes produce final states such as
e\mkern-1.5mue\mkern-2.0mu\mu\text{+jets}
, where one of the two electrons is misreconstructed as a photon.
The
87.5
expected events in the
\textit{Prompt}\ \gamma\ \textit{bkg.}
category are dominated by a
68\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
contribution from
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}
simulation.
Naïvely, these events would be removed in the sample overlap-removal strategy described in
Section

9
, because they appear signal-like and should be estimated from
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
simulation.
However, these particular events from
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}
simulation do not fall in the defined overlap region and therefore passed the overlap-removal checks.
This means that either the
p_{\text{T}}
of the simulated photon is below the defined threshold, or that the simulated photon is too close to a lepton.
In both cases, this type of event is not what is considered
signal
in this analysis, and it should therefore be sorted into the
\textit{Prompt}\ \gamma\ \textit{bkg.}
category instead.
The remaining events in the
\textit{Prompt}\ \gamma\ \textit{bkg.}
category are dominated by a
19\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
contribution from
t\mkern 0.5mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\mkern-1.0muV
simulation, with smaller fractions coming from
W\mkern-4.0mu\gamma
,
Z\mkern-2.0mu\gamma
and diboson simulation.

comparison in the

, the predictions of the

.

control plots for various observables of the

final state are presented in
Figures

and

.

shows the transverse momenta of the electron, of the muon and of the leading jet as well as the jet multiplicity.
