18
Uncertainties on the fiducial phase-space correction

After having obtained a number of measured signal-like events in data, a fiducial cross-section is calculated at parton level as defined in
Equation

38
in
Chapter

6
.
This involves correcting the obtained event yields at reconstruction level for migration and efficiency effects to obtain a value for the measured number of events in the fiducial volume at parton level.
This correction depends on the reconstruction efficiencies and on the fraction of migrated events, which pass the reconstruction-level selection, but do not lie in the fiducial phase-space volume.
Efficiency
\epsilon
, migration fraction
f_{\textrm{out}}
and the correction factor
C
were already determined for the nominal prediction in
Section

11
, which are the values used to determine the fiducial cross-section in
Chapter

8
.

However, the modelling uncertainties introduced in the previous section might show different efficiency and migration effects.
By reweighting all signal modelling templates to the nominal fiducial acceptance, these differences are already included as rate differences in the templates when compared to the distributions of the nominal prediction.
Nonetheless, for each of these alternative models, the reconstruction efficiencies, migration fractions and correction factors can be calculated and can then be compared against the values obtained with the nominal prediction.
This gives additional insight into the importance of each modelling uncertainty, but is irrelevant for the profile likelihood fit or for the extraction and determination of the fiducial cross-section.
As
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
and
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
were generated as separate processes, their systematic variations are evaluated individually, but always considering the impact on the combined values of
\epsilon
,
f_{\textrm{out}}
and
C
.
As before, some of the systematic variations for the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
process were not generated with a full simulation of the
atlas
detector, but with
afii
simulation.
To avoid any dependencies on possible differences between
afii
and full simulation, a separate
afii
simulation of the nominal
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
prediction is used as a reference for the systematic variations.
An overview of all modelling variations, their efficiencies, migration fractions and correction factors is given in
Table

12
.

The dependency on the choice of the renormalisation and factorisation scales
\mu_{R}
and
\mu_{F}
, estimated as described in
Section

17
, shows little relative differences with respect to the nominal prediction.
Less than
\pm$0.2\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}$
difference are obtained when varying the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
factorisation scale, and the effects of the renormalisation scale variations are even smaller.
The
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
scale variations have low impact with relative uncertainties below
\pm$0.05\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}$
.
Alternative showering with
herwig
7 is simulated with
afii
and is, thus, compared against the nominal
afii
simulation of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
and
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
predictions.
The choice of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
parton-shower model shows a large impact with a relative difference of
-$3.3\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}$
, while the alternative
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
parton-shower model differs by
+$0.1\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}$
from the nominal prediction.
Additional studies were performed to investigate the large impact of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
parton-shower model choice.
It was found that the difference in rate between the two samples is mostly introduced by the kinematic requirements on the clustered
b\text{-jets}
at parton level, and by the required distances of these
b\text{-jets}
to other parton-level objects in the
\eta
–
\phi
plane, but not by different photon kinematics in the two samples.
The
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

pythia
8
A14 var3c
tune variations yield an asymmetric impact of
+$1.2\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}$
and
-$0.2\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}$
.
Relative differences from the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

nnpdf
variations, obtained by taking the standard deviation of the one hundred replicas, amount to approximately
\pm$0.14\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}$
, that from the
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
variations to one order of magnitude less, which justifies them being dropped for the fit setup in
Section

17
.

A large relative difference is found for the uncertainty on the
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
parton definition.
As described in the previous section, this uncertainty assumes a doubling of the
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
events in the fiducial volume, while the efficiency
\epsilon
is kept constant.
The resulting relative difference for the correction factor
C
with respect to the nominal predictions amounts to
-$2.75\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}$
.
As this uncertainty is purely based on the definition of the fiducial volume, it is added directly to the uncertainty of the final result in quadrature.
According to
Equation

38
, the dependence of the fiducial cross-section on
C
is inversely proportional, thus, the observed relative difference with a negative sign is added in quadrature to the upper systematic uncertainty of the fiducial cross-section in the following chapter.

Chapter 8
Results

Before proceeding to the results of the fiducial inclusive and differential cross-section measurements, the predicted event yields are re-evaluated with the systematic uncertainties included that were introduced in the previous chapter.
The pruning criteria are applied to reduce the number of nuisance parameters of the model.
However, correlations of the remaining nuisance parameters are only calculated
during
the fit to data and, thus, the pre-fit predictions assume no correlation among them.
16
16

16

In technical terms, this means that the Hessian matrix of second-order partial derivatives of the log-likelihood function with respect to the nuisance parameters is diagonal.
Off-diagonal elements with mixed partial derivatives, i.e.
\partial^{2}\mathcal{L}/\partial\theta_{i}\partial\theta_{j}
with
i\neq j
, are only estimated in the fitting.

The resulting predictions for each simulation category are listed in
Table

13
.
As done for the table in
Chapter

5
with
mc
-statistical uncertainties only, the predictions of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
and
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
categories marked with (*) were scaled in such a way that the total
mc
prediction matches the numbers of reconstructed events in data in each column of the table.
Compared to the table without systematic uncertainties, the uncertainties on the predictions are enlarged significantly.
This is visible in particular for the
h-fake
,
e-fake
and
\textit{Prompt}\ \gamma\ \textit{bkg.}
categories, the uncertainties of which are dominated by the conservative
50\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
normalisation uncertainties introduced in
Section

17
.

The following sections of this chapter summarise the results of the analysis.
Initially, the fitting framework of the fiducial inclusive cross-section measurement was set up exactly as described in the previous chapters of this thesis.
Studies were then performed to test the stability of the fit, the behaviour of the systematic uncertainties and the expected sensitivity of the measurement.
The latter was estimated using
Asimov
pseudo-data.

Section

19
discusses these studies of the fit configuration and the chosen final fit setup.

Section

20
summarises the fit results obtained with the Asimov pseudo-data in this configured setup.

Section

21
details the results of the profile likelihood fit, when the model is fit to
atlas
data, and presents the central results of this work.

Section

22
shows the differential cross-section distributions obtained from unfolding.

19
Studies of the fit configuration

With the strategy for extracting a fiducial inclusive cross-section value from
atlas
data laid out in
Section

12
, and with the uncertainty model introduced in
Chapter

7
, the fit framework can be set up using the
histfactory
package.
Before performing the profile likelihood fit on the
atlas
dataset, various studies of the fit configuration were done.
This includes the studies on the pruning thresholds for systematic uncertainties and on the shape amplification of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}
modelling uncertainties discussed in the previous chapter.
In addition, the stability of the fit procedure, the behaviour of the systematic uncertainties and the expected sensitivity of the measurement were evaluated using an
Asimov
dataset.
As explained before, this set of pseudo-data is created based on the predicted number of total events in
mc
simulation in each bin of the
S_{\text{T}}
distribution and, hence, constitutes a dataset that matches the
mc
predictions perfectly.
Results obtained with Asimov pseudo-data in the final configured fit setup are summarised in
Section

20
.

This section addresses a few caveats of the initial fit setup that were spotted in early fitting tests to Asimov pseudo-data and to
atlas
data.
More specifically, it was noticed that two of the nuisance parameters associated with the modelling of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
signal showed critical behaviour and needed a revision of the strategy:
those of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

pythia
8
A14 var3c
and of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

ps
model uncertainties.
Fits to Asimov pseudo-data showed strong constraints of their post-fit uncertainties, that is, their post-fit uncertainty estimates
\Delta\hat{\theta}
were reduced significantly compared to their prior uncertainties
\Delta\theta
.
This hints towards a possible overestimation of the associated systematic uncertainties prior to the fit.
The same behaviour was seen when fitting to
atlas
data.
In addition, the data fit revealed strong nuisance-parameter pulls, i.e. their post-fit estimates
\hat{\theta}
were far away from their prior central values
\theta_{0}
.
This indicates that the data

?
favours
?
the alternative predictions over the nominal models, the latter of which are associated with the prior central values of the nuisance parameters.

Figure

46
gives a visual overview of the post-fit estimates
\hat{\theta}
for all modelling uncertainties in early tests with
atlas
data.
To allow an easier comparison, the post-fit values
\hat{\theta}
are shifted by
\theta_{0}
and displayed as a fraction of
\Delta\theta
.
While all other modelling uncertainties show post-fit values around zero and error bars that approximately match the width of the green prior-uncertainty band, the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

var3c
and
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

ps
model parameters are not centred and their error bars are visibly shorter than those of the others.

Figure 46
:
Pull distributions of those nuisance parameters associated with the signal modelling in early fits to
atlas
data.
The nuisance parameters of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

var3c
and the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

ps
model uncertainties are strongly pulled and constrained.

The reason for the pulls and constraints can be understood when comparing the nominal and alternative models with the
atlas
data they are fitted against.

Figure

47
shows modified versions of the template control plots presented in the previous chapter:
here, the plots highlight the combined impact of the templates on the total
mc
prediction, and they are compared to data directly.
Again, the integrals of the nominal
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
and
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
predictions are scaled in such a way that the total integral of the
mc
prediction matches the event yields in data.
The systematic templates were scaled with the same factors to allow a direct comparison with the nominal prediction and with data.
Nominal prediction and data deviate visibly in three areas of the
S_{\text{T}}
distribution:
the prediction undershoots and overshoots data in the bins centred at
250\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
and
525\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
, respectively, and the prediction underestimates data for
S_{\text{T}}>$750\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
.
The strong pull of the nuisance parameter associated with the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

ps
model uncertainty is most likely caused by the latter where data agrees more with the down variation shown in blue over the entire range than with the nominal prediction.
In addition, the bin at
250\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
is described better by this variation than by the nominal prediction.
The nominal prediction disagrees with data in the bin at
525\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
, but neither the up nor the down variation show remarkably better agreement with data than the nominal prediction.
Similarly, the pull of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

var3c
nuisance parameter towards the down variation can be explained by the bins at
250\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}525\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
:
in both bins, the down variation in solid blue describes the data spectrum better than the nominal prediction.

Figure 47
:
Combined systematic templates for the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

var3c
and the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

ps
model uncertainties in comparison with data.
The combined templates of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
and
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
categories are scaled in such a way that the total integral of the
mc
prediction matches the event yields in data.
The systematic variations were scaled with the same factors to highlight how they relate to the nominal prediction and to data.

As pulls and constraints are observed for both of these uncertainties, and as both are expected to have a large impact on the sensitivity of the result, it was decided to de-correlate rate and shape effects of their templates.
That is, the parameters
\eta(\theta)
and
\sigma_{b}(\theta)
, reflecting overall normalisation differences and bin-by-bin shape differences, respectively, were included in the fit through two separate nuisance parameters.
This allows both parameters to be treated independently.
In particular, in a de-correlated scenario the overall rate uncertainty cannot be affected by any pulls and constraints due to shape information from the templates.
And while this is a conservative estimate of the impact of these two modelling uncertainties, the observed pulls and constraints can be attributed separately to the rate and shape components.
The expectation here is that both components may develop correlations with other nuisance parameters and the parameter of interest and may be pulled, but only the shape can be constrained through a bin-by-bin comparison with data.

Table

14
shows the effect of removing the rate components from the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

var3c
and
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

ps
model uncertainty templates (which are then included into the fit model with their own nuisance parameters).
The table lists the constraints
\Delta\hat{\theta}/\Delta\theta
observed in a fit to Asimov pseudo-data and the constraints and pulls observed for a fit to
atlas
data.
The arrows point out how pulls and constraints change when removing the rate uncertainty from the templates – hence, effectively comparing combined rate and shape templates to shape-only templates:
in both fits to Asimov and
atlas
data, the constraints are relaxed by
6\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}11\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
for the two nuisance parameters.
In addition, the pull
(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0})/\Delta\theta
observed for the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

var3c
is reduced by almost
50\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
, and that of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

ps
model uncertainty by about
4\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
.
The strong pull reduction for the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

var3c
variations can be explained with
Figure

47
:
as the rate uncertainties are removed, the curves of the down and up variation move up and down by
1.8\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
, respectively.
This yields little difference between the templates in the bin centred at
525\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
, where prediction and data deviate.
Remaining deviations in the bin at
250\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
and for
S_{\text{T}}>$750\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}$
do not favour the same, but opposing variations, thus, resulting in an overall much reduced pull.

In a fit to Asimov pseudo-data, the post-fit constraints,

In total, the sensitivity of the result is expected to decrease with uncorrelated rate and shape components of two dominant modelling uncertainties as the uncorrelated rate components cannot be constrained with shape information.
More side-by-side comparisons of the impact of these changes are shown in

, where this question is also addressed.

20
Fit results with Asimov pseudo-data

Before fitting the configured fit model against
atlas
data, test scenarios with Asimov pseudo-data provide valuable information about the model itself.
The pseudo-data, created directly from the nominal prediction, represents a

?
perfect
?
dataset where none of the nuisance parameters is expected to be pulled from its nominal value
\theta_{0}
, i.e. the post-fit estimate obeys
\hat{\theta}=\theta_{0}
.
The same goes for the parameter of interest: as the spectra of the pseudo-data and of the nominal prediction are identical, the post-fit estimate of the signal strength remains at
1.0
.
However, the log-likelihood is profiled with respect to the nuisance parameters and the impact of the nuisance-parameter variations on the final result is evaluated.
Although no pulls of the nuisance parameters are expected, their post-fit uncertainty estimate
\Delta\hat{\theta}
can still be reduced compared to the prior uncertainty
\Delta\theta
, i.e. the parameter can be constrained.

Table

15
lists all nuisance parameters constrained during a fit to Asimov pseudo-data by more than
10\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
.
The only uncertainties with significant constraints are the shape components of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

var3c
and of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

ps
model variations that were de-correlated from their rate components, c.f.
Section

19
.

Table 15
:
Constrained nuisance parameters in a fit to Asimov pseudo-data.
Only those with post-fit uncertainty estimates
\Delta\hat{\theta}
smaller than
90\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
of the prior uncertainties
\Delta\theta
are listed.
Large constraints are only observed for the shape components of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

var3c
and of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

ps
model variations.
For these two uncertainties, the shape and rate components of the templates were de-correlated as detailed in
Section

19
.

In addition to possible constraints, an Asimov fit scenario also gives an estimate of the correlation values between the model parameters.
A correlation matrix of all nuisance parameters with
10\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
or higher absolute correlation value to others or to the signal strength is presented in
Figure

48
.
Many of the listed nuisance parameters show large (positive or negative) correlation values with the signal strength:
among those are the rate components of the aforementioned signal modelling uncertainties, i.e. the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

var3c
and
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

ps
model rate variations.
Overall normalisation uncertainties of
\pm 50\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
assigned to the
h-fake
and
\textit{Prompt}\ \gamma\ \textit{bkg.}
categories show large negative correlation values with the signal strength as their rates influence the value of
\hat{\mu}
directly.
The same is observed for the luminosity uncertainty: as the luminosity increases, the signal strength decreases, hence a large negative correlation value.
Otherwise noteworthy are the shape components of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

var3c
and of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

ps
model uncertainties that are highly correlated with many of the other listed nuisance parameters.
They are correlated with each other with
48\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
which is the largest overall absolute value of correlation observed.

Figure 48
:
Correlation matrix for the fit to Asimov pseudo-data.
All nuisance parameters with
10\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
or higher absolute correlation value to other parameters or to the signal strength are shown.

Those nuisance parameters with large correlation values to the signal strength are also expected to have a large impact on the sensitivity of the result.
The post-fit impact of each nuisance parameter can be evaluated by performing a fit, where the tested parameter is fixed to its upper and lower post-fit estimates
\hat{\theta}\pm\Delta\hat{\theta}
, while the others remain free.
The same can be done for the pre-fit impact by fixing the parameter to its upper and lower prior values
\theta_{0}\pm\Delta\theta
.
The resulting difference in the signal strength
\Delta\mu
with respect to that obtained in the nominal fit scenario is an estimate of the impact of the tested parameter on the result.
The impact calculation was performed for all nuisance parameters after pruning, the results of which are shown in
Figure

49
.
The plot ranks those nuisance parameters with the highest expected post-fit impact on the signal strength.
The framed blue and turquoise rectangles indicate the pre-fit impact, the solid boxes the post-fit impact.
They are overlaid with the nuisance-parameter values
(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0})/\Delta\theta
post fit.
The highest ranked nuisance parameters are identical with those that show large correlation values to the signal strength in
Figure

48
:
among the top five are the rate components of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

var3c
and
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

ps
model uncertainties, the normalisation uncertainties of the
h-fake
and
\textit{Prompt}\ \gamma\ \textit{bkg.}
categories and the luminosity uncertainty.
Others affecting the result significantly are the uncertainties on the photon identification and isolation efficiencies, on the electron identification efficiency and on the pile-up reweighting, followed by uncertainties on the jet energy-scale calibration.

Figure 49
:
Nuisance parameters ranked according to their impact on the parameter of interest in a fit to Asimov pseudo-data.
The framed blue and turquoise rectangles indicate the pre-fit impact
\Delta\mu
of each nuisance parameter on the signal strength
\mu
, whereas the filled blue and turquoise areas show the post-fit impact.
Possible differences between the two reflect a reduction of the impact due to nuisance-parameter constraints or correlations determined in the fit.
The impact is overlaid with the nuisance-parameter pulls
(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0})/\Delta\theta
, all of which are centred at zero for the Asimov fit.

The Asimov fit also gives an expected uncertainty for the parameter of interest, i.e. the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
signal strength.
The calculated value of the signal strength is

\displaystyle\mu=1.000\pm 0.023\mbox{$\,$(stat.)$\,$}^{+0.065}_{-0.059}\mbox{$\,$(syst.)$\,$}=1.000\,^{+0.069}_{-0.063}\,,

(47)

that is, the measurement is expected to have a sensitivity corresponding to less than
7\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
relative uncertainty.
The first and second quoted uncertainties correspond to statistical and systematic uncertainties, respectively.
The first are estimated in a
stat-only
fit where all nuisance parameters are fixed to their best-fit values and only the statistical uncertainties are evaluated.
Systematic uncertainties are calculated as the quadratic difference between the total uncertainties in the nominal fit scenario and those obtained from the stat-only fit.

21
Fit results with ATLAS data

This section summarises the results of the fit to the Run 2
atlas
dataset as introduced in
Chapter

5
.
The fitting model uses de-correlated rate and shape components of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

var3c
and
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

ps
model variations as discussed in
Section

19
, i.e. it is identical to that used in the previous section to fit Asimov pseudo-data.
This section largely provides the same plots and information to allow a direct comparison with the Asimov fit.

The post-fit estimates
\hat{\theta}
of the nuisance parameters are calculated during the fit as done for Asimov pseudo-data.
In the data fit, in addition to possible constraints, they may also be pulled from their nominal prior values, i.e.
\hat{\theta}\neq\theta_{0}
.
A visual overview of all nuisance parameters and their pulls observed in the fit to
atlas
data is given in
Figure

50
.

Figure 50
:
Nuisance-parameter pulls in the fit to data.
The plot is cut in half at the bottom of the left-hand column and continues on the top right.
The post-fit estimates
\hat{\theta}
are shifted by the central prior value
\theta_{0}
and shown as a fraction of
\Delta\theta
.

As this overview contains some one hundred fifty nuisance parameters, the most strongly pulled and constrained parameters are listed in
Table

16
.
Similarly to the Asimov fit in
Table

15
, the shape components of the two problematic modelling uncertainties, i.e. the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

var3c
and
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

ps
model uncertainties, show large constraints – albeit relaxed with respect to a scenario, where the rate and shape components of these uncertainties remain correlated, c.f.
Section

19
.
Compared to the Asimov fit, the constraints in data are slightly tightened, from values of
73.6\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}75.1\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
to
71.2\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}71.0\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
for the
var3c
and
ps
model variations, respectively.
A large pull, where the post-fit estimate
\hat{\theta}
deviates from the prior nominal value
\theta_{0}
, is observed for the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

ps
model shape variation:
the nuisance parameter is pulled beyond the prior interval of one standard deviation to
(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0})/\Delta\theta=$-1.40$
.
This may be explained with the distribution of the corresponding templates shown in
Section

19
, where this pull was also discussed.
Smaller pulls are observed for the shape-only uncertainty associated to the
ps
model choice for
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}
, and for one of the modelling uncertainties of the jet energy-scale in-situ calibration.

The nuisance parameters largely correlated with the parameter of interest also show a large impact on the uncertainty associated with that parameter.
Pre-fit and post-fit impact values of each nuisance parameter are evaluated the same way as described in
Section

20
:
the nuisance parameter under test is fixed to its pre-fit and post-fit one-standard deviation values, i.e.
\theta_{0}\pm\Delta\theta
and
\hat{\theta}\pm\Delta\hat{\theta}
, and then the fit is redone for all these scenarios.
The resulting impact, given as difference in the obtained signal strength with respect to the nominal fit,
\Delta\mu
, is shown in
Figure

52
.
The framed blue and turquoise rectangles indicate the pre-fit impact, the solid boxes the post-fit impact.
They are overlaid with the nuisance-parameter values
(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{0})/\Delta\theta
post fit.
The highest-ranked nuisance parameters are identical with those of the Asimov fit scenario:
the rate components of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

var3c
and
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

ps
model uncertainties, the normalisation uncertainties of the
h-fake
and
\textit{Prompt}\ \gamma\ \textit{bkg.}
background categories and the luminosity uncertainty are among the top six.
All of them show large correlations with
\mu
in the correlation matrix, c.f.
Figure

51
.
As in the fit to Asimov pseudo-data, others affecting the result are the uncertainties on the photon identification and isolation efficiencies, on the electron identification efficiency and on the pile-up reweighting, all measured in data and corrected in
mc
simulation through scale factors.
None of the highly ranked uncertainties show large pulls or constraints, which underlines the stability of the fit.

:

.

This can also be demonstrated by a scan of the (profiled) log-likelihood.
In an interval of
\hat{\mu}\pm 0.2
around the post-fit estimate of the parameter of interest, the fit is redone with the signal strength
\mu
fixed to
30
equidistant sampling points in that interval.
The resulting value of the log-likelihood is then compared against the value obtained in the nominal fit scenario and their difference
\Delta\ln(\mathcal{L})
is calculated for each sampling point.
The result of this log-likelihood scan is shown in
Figure

53
.
Over the entire scanned spectrum of the signal strength, the fit shows high stability and there are no hints towards local minima or other artefacts that could point towards problems in the likelihood function.

Figure 53
:
Scan of the profile likelihood in the fit to data for the parameter of interest.
The
1\sigma
interval, defined by
-\Delta\ln(\mathcal{L})=0.5
and quoted as an uncertainty on the result, is marked with dashed lines.
The fit shows high stability over the entire scanned range.

The post-fit value of the signal strength measured with the
atlas
dataset amounts to

\displaystyle\mu=1.411\,^{+0.028}_{-0.027}\mbox{$\,$(stat.)$\,$}^{+0.084}_{-0.077}\mbox{$\,$(syst.)$\,$}=1.411\,^{+0.089}_{-0.082}\,,

(48)

corresponding to total relative uncertainties of
{}^{+$6.3\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}$}_{-$5.8\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}$}
.
The first and second quoted uncertainties correspond to statistical and systematic uncertainties, respectively.
The first are estimated in a
stat-only
fit where all nuisance parameters are fixed to their best-fit values and only the post-fit statistical uncertainties are evaluated.
Systematic uncertainties are calculated as the quadratic difference between the total uncertainties in the nominal fit scenario and those obtained from the stat-only fit.
As the obtained central value deviates from
1.0
, the naïve conclusion would be an incompatibility with the
sm
prediction.
However, the predictions of the signal processes only use
lo
cross-sections in
qcd
, while large
nlo
corrections are expected, c.f.
Chapters

4
and
5
.
Thus, deviating values are anticipated when fitting the model against
atlas
data and are not problematic.

The post-fit distribution of
S_{\text{T}}
is shown in
Figure

54
on the right-hand side, where it can be compared directly to its pre-fit distribution.
In comparison, the post-fit distribution shows a much narrower hatched uncertainty band for the total prediction.
On the one hand, this is due to the obtained constraints of some of the nuisance parameters.
On the other hand, the log-likelihood is now profiled with respect to all nuisance parameters and, hence, correlations between the parameters are taken into account.
In technical terms, the Hessian matrix of second-order derivatives of the log-likelihood has non-zero off-diagonal elements post-fit.
Strongly pronounced deviations in the pre-fit distributions, e.g. those in the bins centred at
525\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}1100\text{\,}\mathrm{GeV}
, are compensated for by the fit and the overall agreement between the total prediction and data is improved.

Figure 54
:
Comparison of pre-fit and post-fit distributions of the
S_{\text{T}}
observable.
The agreement between post-fit prediction and data was evaluated with a Pearson
\chi^{2}
test and amounts to
\chi^{2}/\text{{ndf} }=21.2/14
.
This corresponds to a
p
-value of
0.10
.

Using the best-fit values of all nuisance parameters and the post-fit estimate of the signal strength, the predicted event yields can be recalculated.
The obtained values are shown in
Table

17
and are compared to the pre-fit yields as listed in
Table

13
for the total Run 2 dataset.
The values show little to no change in the central values from pre-fit to post-fit, thus, none of the background predictions is adjusted drastically in the profile likelihood fit.
Instead, the scaling applied to the predictions of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
and
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
categories marked with (*) for the pre-fit values has approximately the same effect as the fitting of the free-floating signal-strength parameter that controls these categories.
The uncertainties associated with the background categories show no large changes from pre-fit to post-fit, but the pre-fit uncertainties of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
-related categories are reduced considerably.
Due to cross-correlations between the uncertainties of the individual event categories, the total
mc
prediction also shows strongly reduced post-fit uncertainties.

and

, the pre-fit yields are rounded to significant digits.

With the post-fit predictions of the event yields, the fiducial inclusive cross-section value can be calculated.
This is done using
Equation

38
which avoids a direct dependence of the cross-section on the signal strength by considering the ratio of signal-like
e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
-channel events to all signal-like events.
With this equation, the fiducial cross-section is

\displaystyle\sigma^{\text{fid}}(t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma\to e\mkern-2.0mu\mu)=39.6\pm 0.8\mbox{$\,$(stat.)$\,$}^{+2.6}_{-2.2}\mbox{$\,$(syst.)$\,$}$\mathrm{fb}$=39.6\,^{+2.7}_{-2.3}\;$\mathrm{fb}$\,,

(49)

corresponding to total relative uncertainties of
{}^{+$6.9\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}$}_{-$5.8\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}$}
.
The statistical uncertainties are propagated directly from the statistical uncertainties on the signal strength in
Equation

48
.
For the systematic uncertainties, as detailed in
Section

18
, the uncertainties associated with the
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
parton definition were added in quadrature to the upper uncertainties.
This explains the different relative uncertainties of the signal strength in
Equation

48
and of the cross-section in
Equation

49
.
The measured cross-section is in good agreement with the value predicted by the fixed-order theory computation
[
30
,
31
]
in
Equation

45
, which expects a value of
\sigma^{\text{fid}}_{\textsc{nlo}}=38.50\,^{+0.56}_{-2.18}\,(\text{scale})\,^{+1.04}_{-1.18}\,(\text{{pdf} })\,$\mathrm{fb}$
.

As systematics are the dominant source of uncertainties for this measurement, their composition is of particular interest.
While the ranked nuisance parameters in
Figure

52
give an idea about which of the individual parameters have the largest impact on the result, classifying the parameters into groups of uncertainty sources gives a more qualitative picture of the uncertainty composition.

Table

18
lists the impact on the signal strength
\Delta\mu
with respect to the signal-strength uncertainty in the nominal fit scenario for a total of nine groups of uncertainties.
As done for the total systematic uncertainties, the impact values are obtained by fixing the group of nuisance parameters to their best-fit values.
The computed uncertainties on
\mu
are then subtracted in quadrature from those of the nominal fit scenario, which yields an estimate of the group’s impact.
The quoted values in
Table

18
are averaged for upper and lower uncertainties obtained with this method.

The nuisance parameters for modelling uncertainties are grouped into signal and background modelling to disentangle these two.
Uncertainties on the signal-process modelling have the largest impact on the result with

.
Background modelling, which also includes the
50\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}

h-fake
,

and

.
Uncertainties associated with the reconstruction, identification and calibration of physics objects, c.f.

, are grouped into separate categories for each type of object, e.g. photon uncertainties.

In addition, the luminosity uncertainty limits the sensitivity of the measurement significantly, as also seen in the ranking plot.
This single-nuisance-parameter group has a total impact of
1.8\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
.
Other uncertainties include those on the pile-up and on flavour-tagging, whereas statistical limitations of the used
mc

In addition, the
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
parton-definition uncertainty, added in quadrature to the final result, limits the sensitivity.

22
Differential cross-section measurements

Fiducial differential cross-section measurements and their associated methodology were not the main focus of the author’s work, but they provide valuable complementary results to the fiducial inclusive cross-section measurement.
Thus, this section briefly summarises the results obtained in the fiducial differential cross-section measurements in Ref.
[
2
]
17
17

17

After this thesis had been finalised and submitted in its first version, minor adjustments to the smoothing and symmetrisation of systematics were made by the
atlas
Collaboration for the publication of the results in Ref.
[
2
]
.
This section was now updated to summarise the published results of Ref.
[
2
]
.
Thus, the results of this section are not fully consistent with the treatment described in
Chapter

7
, although no visible difference in the results or in the associated tests is observed.

.
The five observables,
i.e. the transverse momentum and absolute pseudorapidity of the photon,
the distance
\Delta R_{\text{min}}(\gamma,\ell)
in the
\eta
–
\phi
plane between the photon and the closer of the two charged leptons,
and the absolute differences in pseudorapidities
\left|\Delta\eta(\ell,\ell)\right|
and in azimuthal angles
\Delta\phi(\ell,\ell)
between the two charged leptons,
are first optimised in their binning.
Non-equidistant binning is used to overcome large statistical uncertainties in less populated areas of the observable distributions.
Two criteria are considered for the binning of these unfolding observables:
firstly, the statistical uncertainty in each bin of the distributions ought to be less than
10\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
.
This ensures a minimum number of predicted events per bin.
Secondly, the bin width has to be larger than twice the expected resolution of the variable to minimise migration effects during unfolding.

As introduced in
Section

13
, the binning is then stress-tested using the bootstrap method:
one thousand sets of bootstrap replicas are generated from
mc
simulation using Poisson bin-by-bin fluctuations.
Each of these sets, generated with the expected statistical uncertainty in data, is unfolded with the same unfolding setup.
Then, for the ensemble of one thousand sets, the pulls in each bin with respect to the truth distributions are calculated.
These pulls should be unbiased, i.e. centred around zero, and their average spread should follow that of a Gaussian distribution with a width equal to the expected statistical uncertainty in that bin.
All five observables show high stability in these tests with the chosen binning.
The pull tests are followed by two stress tests related to the shape stability of the observables:
the nominal unfolding setups are used to unfold observable spectra that are reweighted in their shape to assess whether the setups are biased towards the shapes they were created with.
The first reweighting introduces linear slopes to the pseudo-data, the other enhances bin-by-bin differences between the reconstructed spectra in
mc
simulation and
atlas
data.
In both stress tests, the nominal unfolding setups recover the used truth distributions for all five observables, thus, certifying high stability of the setups.

presents measured differential cross-sections as a function of the photon transverse momentum and of the difference in azimuthal angles
\Delta\phi(\ell,\ell)
of the two charged leptons.
The plots allow a direct comparison of the unfolded
atlas
data with the fixed-order theory computation
[
30
,
31
]
, which show little difference in both spectra.
Statistical as well as combined statistical and systematic uncertainties on the unfolded spectra are represented by the differently shaded uncertainty bands, respectively.
The agreement can also be calculated with a Pearson
\chi^{2}
test.

shows the obtained
\chi^{2}

\chi^{2}

p

nlo

p

Chapter 9
Summary and conclusions

The electromagnetic coupling between the top quark and the photon is accessible through the measurement of
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
production, where a top-quark pair is produced in association with a hard photon.
Both strength and structure of the coupling are sensitive to physics beyond the Standard Model.
This thesis presents results of
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
cross-section measurements using data recorded with the
atlas
detector at the
lhc
at
cern
.
The examined dataset was taken during Run 2 of the
lhc
in the years 2015 to 2018 in proton-proton collisions at a centre-of-mass energy of
13\text{\,}\mathrm{T}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}
and corresponds to an integrated luminosity of
139\text{\,}\mathrm{fb^{-1}}
.
The analysis focuses on the
e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
final state due to its high signal purity and precise available theory predictions
[
30
,
31
]
.
In order to be consistent with these predictions that also include non-doubly-resonant diagrams, a combined measurement of
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma+t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
is performed.
The signal region of the measurement is defined by selecting events with exactly one photon, one electron and one muon of opposite electric charge, and at least two jets, of which one or more must be
b\text{-tagged}
.

With a signal-to-background ratio of almost
6:1
, only very few predicted background events contaminate the chosen signal region.
Other decay channels of the signal processes that migrated into the
e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
selection constitute the largest fraction.
This includes indirect
e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
final states through the decay of one or two
\tau
-leptons and a small fraction of lepton+jets events with an additional fake lepton.
Other background processes are categorised into those containing h-fake photons, e-fake photons and prompt photons.
Due to their low contributions, the rate of fake photons is estimated from
mc
simulation and no data-driven corrections are applied to the estimate.
Instead, all three background categories are assigned conservative normalisation uncertainties.

The focus of this thesis lies on the measurement of the fiducial inclusive cross-section.

atlas
data is compared with
mc
simulation in a binned distribution of the observable
S_{\text{T}}
, defined as the sum of transverse momenta of all objects of the event, including
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
.
The signal strength of the combined
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma+t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
production is estimated through a maximum likelihood fit to data.
Systematic uncertainties on the predictions are included into the fitting procedure using the template method and the profiling technique.
The results are then corrected for efficiency and migration effects with respect to a fiducial phase-space volume at parton level that is defined in such a way that it mimics the experimental cuts of the signal region.
The fit yields a fiducial cross-section of

\displaystyle\sigma^{\text{fid}}(t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma\to e\mkern-2.0mu\mu)=39.6\pm 0.8\mbox{$\,$(stat.)$\,$}^{+2.6}_{-2.2}\mbox{$\,$(syst.)$\,$}$\mathrm{fb}$=39.6\,^{+2.7}_{-2.3}\;$\mathrm{fb}$\,,

(50)

corresponding to total relative uncertainties of
{}^{+$6.9\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}$}_{-$5.8\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}$}
.
The authors of Ref.
[
30
,
31
]
provide a dedicated computation of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
cross-section at
nlo
in
qcd
in the identical fiducial phase space.
The measured value is in good agreement with the computed fiducial cross-section of
\sigma^{\text{fid}}_{\textsc{nlo}}=38.50\,^{+0.56}_{-2.18}\,(\text{scale})\,^{+1.04}_{-1.18}\,(\text{{pdf} })\,$\mathrm{fb}$
.
Compared to previous results, this constitutes the most precise measurement of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
production cross-section to date.

Figure

58
picks up the previous results shown in
Figure

14
and adds the result of this measurement to the diagram.
While the previous
atlas
measurement in the dilepton channels performed with
36\text{\,}\mathrm{fb^{-1}}
at
13\text{\,}\mathrm{TeV}

[
1
]
was limited by statistical and systematic uncertainties equally, the result presented here shows significantly lower statistical limitations.

Figure 58
:
This measurement in comparison with previous results of
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
production at hadron colliders
[
164
,
165
,
166
,
167
,
1
]
.
As a figure of merit, the measured cross-section over
sm
prediction is given.
The inner error bars are statistical uncertainties only, the outer bars are combined statistical and systematic uncertainties.
The grey blocks represent theory uncertainties.

The statistical uncertainties decreased from
3.8\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
to
1.9\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
, whereas relative systematic uncertainties remain approximately identical despite a conservative treatment of modelling uncertainties in this measurement.
Statistical and systematic uncertainties combined result in an overall uncertainty reduction.
Exploiting the full Run 2 dataset corresponding to an integrated luminosity of
139\text{\,}\mathrm{fb^{-1}}
, this measurement in the
e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
channel has reached the same statistical precision as the lepton+jets channels at
36\text{\,}\mathrm{fb^{-1}}
, while the total uncertainties are some
20\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
lower.
Apart from the improvements on the measurement side, the dedicated fixed-order computation
[
30
,
31
]
is a milestone in terms of precision of
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
theory predictions.

Besides the fiducial inclusive measurement, the ATLAS data is unfolded to parton level using an iterative technique based on Bayes’ theorem.
Measurements of differential cross-sections as functions of the transverse momentum of the photon and of the difference in azimuthal angles
\Delta\phi(\ell,\ell)
between the two leptons are presented.
Three additional differential cross-sections as functions of
\left|\eta(\gamma)\right|
,
\Delta R_{\text{min}}(\gamma,\ell)
and
\left|\Delta\eta(\ell,\ell)\right|
are summarised in
Chapter

13
.
All differential distributions show agreement with the fixed-order theory computation in the examined fiducial phase-space volume.
However,
mc
simulations using matrix-element generators at leading order in
qcd
interfaced to parton-shower algorithms deviate significantly from
atlas
data in some of the spectra.
This is particularly pronounced in the
\Delta\phi(\ell,\ell)
distribution, which is sensitive to spin-correlation effects.
The disagreement between
mc
simulation and
atlas
data in
\Delta\phi(\ell,\ell)
shows trends that are similar to those in unfolded
\Delta\phi(\ell,\ell)
distributions of
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}
production in the
e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
final state
[
277
]
.

An additional focus of this thesis is placed on studies concerning machine-learning techniques to identify photons in the
36\text{\,}\mathrm{fb^{-1}}
analysis performed by
atlas
.
The developed tool, the
ppt
, provides binary classification of photon candidates into prompt photons and h-fake photons based on the lateral and longitudinal evolution of photon showers in the
atlas
calorimeters.
In the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
analysis it is used as an input to an event-level neural network in the lepton+jets channels and provides large separation power between the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
signal and background processes with h-fake photons.

outlook.

Rapid developments in the area of machine learning over the past fifteen years have not spared the field of experimental particle physics.
The
ppt
was one of the first tools on analysis level within
atlas
to use novel open-source machine-learning libraries, such as
keras

[
219
]
and
tensorflow

[
220
]
.
Since then, these have become increasingly popular and are used in various places of the
atlas
software infrastructure.
For example, while the results presented here still rely on the
mv2
high-level algorithm for
b\text{-tagging}
, the
atlas
Collaboration is slowly moving towards a new deep-learning-based tagger named
dl1
that was trained with
keras
and the
theano
library as backend
[
278
]
.
With a plethora of new techniques and tools at hand, these will change the way statistical data analysis is carried out in experimental particle physics fundamentally over the next years.

For the presented
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
measurement in the
e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
final state, the
ppt
or a similar tool was of little interest for this particular channel due to the low background contamination.
However, with the full Run 2 dataset available, precise measurements of
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
final states in the lepton+jets channels would profit from such a tool and make it a tool worth re-investing into.
Various improvements could be considered to boost the overall performance of the
ppt
.
Corrections implemented through simulation-to-data scale factors were needed in
Chapter

3
to apply the
ppt
to
mc
samples with very different photon kinematics than those of the training data.
These problems could be circumvented by retraining the
ppt
using results from the field of domain adaption:
two neural networks could compete in an
adversarial
structure
[
279
,
280
,
281
,
282
]
, where the discriminating model is advised by another in order to remain uncorrelated with kinematic variables of the photon, i.e. with
p_{\text{T}}(\gamma)
and
\left|\eta(\gamma)\right|
.
This could help to adapt to unseen data with different photon kinematics as shown in studies performed within
atlas

[
283
]
.
On the other hand, a machine-learning tool can only be as good as its input data.
One of the limiting factors of deep-learning structures based on photon shower-shape variables remains the poor modelling of such in
mc
simulation.
As discussed in
Section

6
, fudge factors are used to mitigate discrepancies in the peak positions of these variables.
While this is sufficient for particle identification based on one-dimensional cuts on these observables, deep neural networks exploit correlations and non-linear relations between these observables.
With a limited understanding of the photon shower development from
mc
simulation, tools such as the
ppt
will remain constrained by systematic uncertainty estimates needed to close the residual gaps between simulation and data.

The measurement in the
e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
channel presented here set a new benchmark for fiducial inclusive cross-section measurements and will probably not be outperformed by any of the other channels for mainly two reasons:
firstly, it provides the best signal-to-background ratio, while other channels have larger background contamination, thus, relying on more sophisticated estimation techniques and their associated systematic uncertainties to control them.
Secondly, despite the small branching ratio of the
e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
channel, the Run 2 dataset has already provided enough statistics to render a systematically limited measurement.
However, measurements in the
e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
channel itself could yet be improved, e.g. by understanding and reducing its dominant systematic uncertainties.
The ranking of nuisance parameters in
Figure

52
and the impact of groups of systematic uncertainties listed in
Table

18
both point the finger at signal modelling as the most dominant uncertainty source.
The limitations are manifold:
on the one hand, the
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
final state was measured in combination with
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
for the first time, but only
lo
simulations in
qcd
in the five-flavour scheme were available for
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
when this analysis was performed.
This required the introduction of an additional
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma

parton definition
uncertainty detailed in
Section

18
due to the expected, but often missing second
b\text{-quark}
at parton level.
The assigned uncertainty has considerable impact on the measurement precision.
A
lo
+
ps
simulation of the
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-2.0mub\gamma
final state would resolve the observed problems with the parton definition as the second
b\text{-quark}
is generated explicitly in the matrix-element simulation.
19
19

19

As discussed earlier, the leading-order diagram of
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-2.0mub\gamma
is equivalent to the lowest-order diagram for
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
production in the four-flavour scheme, where the
b\text{-quark}
is not described by a
pdf
.

On the other hand, the
lo
+
ps
simulation of the doubly-resonant
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
production shows large associated uncertainties, when varying radiation parameters of the parton shower (
pythia
8
A14 var3c
eigentune) or varying the overall parton-shower model (
pythia
8 vs.
herwig
7).
Significant differences are observed in both rate and shape of the
S_{\text{T}}
distribution.
Including
nlo
effects in
qcd
into the matrix-element calculation could potentially alleviate these differences.
The parton-shower algorithm would then have to be matched to the
nlo
matrix element, but would only have to take care of radiation beyond the
nlo
real-emission corrections – providing an overall more precise simulation of the doubly-resonant
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
final state.
Variations of parton-shower radiation parameters or of the overall parton-shower model would be expected to have a smaller impact on the prediction.
Compared to the fixed-order theory calculation, the ultimate
mc
simulation would be a matrix-element calculation of the
W\mkern-2.0mub\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-2.0mub\gamma
final state at
lo
or even
nlo
in
qcd
, which includes all resonant and non-resonant diagrams and their interference effects into one single simulated
mc
sample.
A similar computation was implemented into
powheg-box
for combined
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}+t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mub
simulation
[
284
,
285
]
and has shown good agreement with unfolded
atlas
data in regions that were designed to be sensitive to interference effects
[
130
]
.

All in all, the
atlas
Run 2 dataset provides more opportunities for precision measurements of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
process.
Although not expected to surpass the
e\mkern-2.0mu\mu
channel in precision, the other channels can provide valuable complementary measurements.
With much larger branching ratios, the lepton+jets channels have enough data available to enable high-resolution differential measurements, possibly even doubly-differential, e.g. differential cross-sections as functions of kinematic variables, such as photon or lepton transverse momenta, and of the jet or
b\text{-jet}
multiplicity.
With many
atlas
calibrations and efficiency measurements improved over the course of Run 2, systematic uncertainties are expected to be reduced considerably with respect to the results obtained with
36\text{\,}\mathrm{fb^{-1}}
.
Furthermore, studies within a MSc thesis project in Göttingen have revealed the potential of machine-learning techniques using multiclass classification at event level in the lepton+jets channels
[
286
]
.
With the possibility to discriminate not only signal and background, but signal and different classes of background processes, such tools could outperform the event-level classifier used in the analysis of 2015 and 2016 data and provide additional separation power through multiple output nodes.
Another MSc thesis project investigated possibilities to enhance the fraction of photons radiated directly by one of the top quarks in
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
production with machine-learning techniques, and has shown promising results
[
287
]
.
Suppressing photons from the
b\text{-quarks}
, the
W\mkern-2.0mu
bosons and the decay products of the
W\mkern-2.0mu
bosons would increase the sensitivity of a
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
measurement to the top-photon coupling, which in turn would allow more thorough tests of its strength and structure.

Eventually, the focus of properties measurements in the top-quark sector will shift from
sm
precision tests to interpreting results in the view of
bsm
theories.
One powerful tool at hand is the
eft
framework, in which multiple theories may be tested simultaneously through their interpretations as effective theories that modify coupling behaviours.
As introduced in
Section

3
,
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
production is sensitive to some of the lowest-order, dimension-6
eft
operators that involve the top quark.
Fiducial inclusive
atlas
measurements, but even more so differential distributions enable constraints of the Wilson coefficients that describe the magnitude of these operators in an
eft
-extended
sm
Lagrangian.
Such measurements require dedicated
mc
simulations with modified coupling behaviours that reflect changes in individual
eft
operators.
Then, templates of observable distributions can be generated for these
eft
-extended predictions and can be compared to (unfolded)
atlas
data as done in this measurement with
sm
predictions.
With the full Run 2 dataset recorded with the
atlas
detector at hand, the lepton+jets channels already provide enough statistics to allow interpreting
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
measurements in the context of
eft
.
This will become even more relevant with the scheduled Run 3 of the
lhc
, which expects proton-proton collisions from 2021 and an estimated integrated luminosity of
300\text{\,}\mathrm{fb^{-1}}
.

Particular attention should also be given to cross-section ratios of
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
and
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}
production, where
\mathcal{R}=\sigma(t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma)/\sigma(t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut})
.
The authors of Ref.
[
31
]
argue that this ratio shows higher stability against radiative corrections and reduced dependence on the choice of renormalisation and factorisation scales and on
pdf
s
.
In addition, measurements of
\mathcal{R}
instead of direct
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
cross-section measurements would yield reduced experimental uncertainties.
The luminosity uncertainty, one of the limitations of the measurement presented in this thesis, would be cancelled in the ratio.
Uncertainties associated with physics objects common to the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
and
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}
final states, e.g. uncertainties on the energy and
p_{\text{T}}
calibrations of leptons and jets, would be reduced considerably.
Such ratio measurements could be performed differentially as well:
distributions such as

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\Delta\phi(\ell,\ell)}=\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\sigma(t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma)}{\mathrm{d}\left(\Delta\phi(\ell,\ell)\right)}\right)\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\sigma(t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut})}{\mathrm{d}\left(\Delta\phi(\ell,\ell)\right)}\right)^{-1}

(51)

could give further insight into the observed shape discrepancies in the
\Delta\phi(\ell,\ell)
observable in both
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
and
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}
production, while reducing theoretical and experimental uncertainties to a minimum with respect to direct cross-section measurements.
The magnitude of these ratios would be sensitive to the top-photon coupling strength directly, and differential distributions of
\mathcal{R}
could be used to probe
atlas
data for modified coupling structures.

Chapter 12
Fit results in the non-configured setup

Section

19
discusses issues with the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

var3c
and
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

ps
model variations, and for the final results presented in
Sections

20
and
21
, the fit was configured to use the following setup:
the rate and shape components of the two modelling uncertainties were de-correlated due to strong pulls and constraints.
This appendix now provides some additional plots for the correlated fit scenario that was not chosen for the final fit setup.
Similarly to what is done in
Section

20
, the expected uncertainty for the parameter of interest can be calculated in an Asimov fit scenario. The obtained value is

\displaystyle\mu=1.000\pm 0.023\mbox{$\,$(stat.)$\,$}^{+0.055}_{-0.051}\mbox{$\,$(syst.)$\,$}=1.000\,^{+0.060}_{-0.056}\,,

(52)

thus, the expected statistical uncertainties are identical to those predicted in
Equation

47
, but the expected systematic uncertainties are reduced by approximately
15\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
compared to the de-correlated scenario.
A fit to data yields a signal strength of

\displaystyle\mu=1.357\,^{+0.027}_{-0.026}\mbox{$\,$(stat.)$\,$}^{+0.064}_{-0.062}\mbox{$\,$(syst.)$\,$}=1.357\,^{+0.069}_{-0.067}\,,

(53)

corresponding to total relative uncertainties of
{}^{+$5.2\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}$}_{-$4.9\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}$}
.
Thus, removing the de-correlation leads to a down shift of the signal strength by
0.054
and a reduction of the total relative uncertainties by approximately
15\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
.
The resulting post-fit yields are listed in
Table

21
, equivalent to
Table

17
in the main body for the de-correlated scenario.
Despite the down shift of the signal strength, which is reflected in the reduced post-fit
t\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-3.0mu\gamma
yields, the post-fit
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma
yields remain unchanged with respect to the scenario in the main body.
This is because the reduced signal strength is compensated by the strongly pulled
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

ps
model variation.
Translated into a fiducial inclusive cross-section value using
Equation

38
, this yields

\displaystyle\mu^{\text{fid}}(t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma\to e\mkern-2.0mu\mu)=39.6\pm 0.8\mbox{$\,$(stat.)$\,$}^{+2.2}_{-1.8}\mbox{$\,$(syst.)$\,$}$\mathrm{fb}$=39.6\,^{+2.3}_{-2.0}\;$\mathrm{fb}$\,,

(54)

corresponding to total relative uncertainties of
{}^{+$5.8\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}$}_{-$4.9\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}$}
.
Compared to the final result in the main body in
Equation

49
, the central value of the measured fiducial inclusive cross-section is
unchanged
, but the systematic and total uncertainties are decreased by approximately
15\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
.
This is reassuring as the decisions made about the fit setup have no impact on the central values of the result, but only affect the uncertainty estimate.

The ranking plots show that the rate component of the
ps
model, i.e. the highest-ranked nuisance parameter in the fit results in the main body, has a weaker impact on the result when correlated with its shape information.
It is only ranked the fourth-highest nuisance parameter in this section.
In values, the post-fit impact of the rate-only component in the main body was calculated to be
\Delta\mu=^{+0.045}_{-0.043}
for the fit to data.
The post-fit impact of the combined rate and shape uncertainty in this section is
\Delta\mu=^{+0.018}_{-0.020}
in the data fit.
This is the reason for the decreased relative uncertainties of the result presented here.

However, this scenario puts a lot more importance on the shape information of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

ps
model variation as it remains correlated with the rate.
Through comparison with the de-correlated scenario it becomes clear that the shape component is what causes the large pull and constraint of the combined nuisance parameter.
Looking at the template distributions in
Figure

47
, it was discussed that the large pull of the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

ps
model is due to discrepancies between data and nominal
mc
prediction.
In the de-correlated scenario of the main body, this is compensated by the shape-only nuisance parameter – a parameter that has negligible impact on the parameter of interest and is not ranked among the top twenty high-impact parameters.
Its post-fit impact is calculated to be
|\Delta\mu|<0.004
.
In the correlated scenario, however, the discrepancy is compensated for by the combined rate-and-shape nuisance parameter, which has a major impact on the parameter of interest.
The compensation of this discrepancy through the
t\mkern 1.0mu\bar{\mkern-1.0mut}\gamma

ps
model shape component is expected to only be an artefact of the model, i.e. it is simply that template in the fit model which compensates these discrepancies

?
best
?
.
Hence, it is much preferable to have this compensation done by a lowly ranked nuisance parameter that has little influence on the final result, although this increases the overall uncertainties of the final result.
